Shifted convolution sums of coefficients of symmetric power L L -functions with k k -full kernels over sums of squares in arithmetic progressions
Jewel Mahajan, Arnab Mitra

Abstract
. Let q q be an integer and let f f be a normalised Hecke eigenform of integral weight for the full modular group. Let L ⁡ ( s , sym j ​ f ) L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{j}f) denote the j j -th symmetric power L L -function associated to f f , and let λ sym j ​ f ​ ( n ) \lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n) denote its n n -th coefficient. We study the behaviour of the partial sum of λ sym j ​ f ​ ( n ) \lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n) , and of its second moment, taken over those sums of m m squares that are congruent to 1 1 modulo q q . As an application, we investigate the shifted convolution sum of λ sym j ​ f ​ ( n ) \lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n) against a k k -full kernel function, for any k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 . We also study the number of sign changes of λ sym j ​ f ​ ( n ) \lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n) twisted with a k k -full kernel function, again over sums of m m squares. Throughout, m m is even with m ∈ { 2 , 4 , 6 , 8 , 10 , 12 } m\in\{2,4,6,8,10,12\} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11M36, 11M41, 11M06

1.
Introduction

Let
k\geq 2
be any integer. An integer valued function
a(n)
is called a
k
-full function if
p^{k}|a(n)
whenever
p|a(n)
. Note that any positive integer
n1
can be uniquely decomposed into
n=a_{1}(n)a_{2}(n)
with
(a_{1}(n),a_{2}(n))=1
, where
a_{1}(n)
is
k
-free and
a_{2}(n)
is
k
-full. A non-negative integer valued function
a(n)
is called a
k
-full kernel function if
a(n)=a(a_{2}(n))
for all
n\geq 1
and
a(n)\ll n^{\epsilon}
for all
\epsilon>0
. The notion of a
k
-full kernel function is given by Ivić and Tenenbaum
[
7
]
. Note that
k
-full kernel functions are not necessarily multiplicative.

Let
S_{\kappa}
be the space of all holomorphic cusp forms of integer weight
\kappa
for the full modular group
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
and
f\in S_{\kappa}
. Let
\lambda_{f}(n)
be the normalised
n
-th Fourier coefficient of the Fourier expansion of
f(z)
at the cusp
\infty
, i.e.,

f(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{f}(n)n^{\frac{\kappa-1}{2}}e^{2\pi inz},

where
\Im(z)>0
. Then the
L
-function attached to
\lambda_{f}(n)
is defined as

L(s,f)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{f}(n)}{n^{s}}

for
\Re(s)>1
, where
\lambda_{f}(n)
are the eigenvalues of all the Hecke operators
T_{n}
.

In 1974, Deligne
[
2
]
proved that for any prime
p
, there exist complex numbers
\alpha(p)
and
\beta(p)
such that

(1)

\displaystyle\alpha(p)\beta(p)

\displaystyle=1,

(2)

\displaystyle|\alpha(p)|=|\beta(p)|

\displaystyle=1.

Then
L(s,f)
can be written as

L(s,f)=\prod_{p}\left(1-\frac{\alpha(p)}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}\left(1-\frac{\beta(p)}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1},

where the product runs over all primes
p
. Also,
|\lambda_{f}(n)|\leq d(n)
, where
d(n)
is the divisor function.

The symmetric square
L
-function is defined as

L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{2}f):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{2}f}(n)}{n^{s}}=\prod_{p}\left(1-\frac{\alpha^{2}(p)}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}\left(1-\frac{\beta^{2}(p)}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}

for
\Re(s)>1
, where
\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{2}f}(n)
is multiplicative.

In
1987
, Erdős and Ivić
[
3
]
proved that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}a(n)d(n+1)=C_{1}x\log x+C_{2}x+O(x^{\frac{8}{9}+\epsilon})

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}a(n)w(n+1)=D_{1}x\log\log x+D_{2}x+O\left(\frac{x}{\log x}\right),

where
d(n)
is the divisor function, and
w(n)
is the number of different prime factors of
n
, and
C_{1}>0
,
D_{1}>0
,
C_{2}
,
D_{2}
are constants that can be evaluated explicitly.

In
[
13
]
, Lü and Wang investigated the shifted convolution
sums of squares of Fourier coefficients with a
2
-full kernel function
a(n)
and obtained an asymptotic
formula for the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}a(n)\lambda^{2}_{f}(n+1).

Later, Venkatasubbareddy and Sankaranarayanan
[
12
]
extended the problem to fourth moments, and generalised to
k
-full kernel functions. For
q\geq 100
and
q\ll x^{\frac{23}{181}-\epsilon}
, they proved that

(3)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x+1\\
n\equiv 1~(\mathrm{mod}~q)\end{subarray}}\lambda_{f}^{4}(n)=c_{1}x\log x\frac{\phi(q)}{q^{2}}+O\left(\frac{x^{\frac{158}{181}+\epsilon}q^{1+\epsilon}}{\phi(q)}\right)

and

for
q\geq 100
and
q\ll x^{\frac{3}{23}-\epsilon}
, they proved that

(4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x+1\\
n\equiv 1~(\mathrm{mod}~q)\end{subarray}}\lambda_{f}^{4}(n)=c_{1}x\log x\frac{\phi(q)}{q^{2}}+O\left(\frac{x^{\frac{20}{23}+\epsilon}q^{1+\epsilon}}{\phi(q)}\right).

As an application, for a
k
-full kernel function
a(n)
, they proved that

(5)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}a(n)\lambda_{f}^{4}(n+1)=c_{2}x\log x+O\left(x^{\frac{520k+23}{543k}+\epsilon}\right).

On the other hand, Wang
[
14
]
proved that for a
k
-full kernel function
a(n)
and
f\in S_{\kappa}
,

(6)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}a(n)\lambda^{2}_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n+1)=C_{f,j}x+O\left(x^{1-\frac{2k-2}{3k(j+1)^{2}}+\epsilon}\right),

and

(7)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}a(n)\lambda^{3}_{\mathrm{sym}^{2}f}(n+1)=Dx+O\left(x^{\frac{1805k+46}{1851k}+\epsilon}\right).

This article investigates the behaviour of the partial sum of
\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n)
and its second moment for a sequence of integers
n
which are expressible as a sum of
m
squares, which are
1
modulo
q
, where
q
is an integer. As an application, we examine their shifted convolution sum with a weight function, which is a
k
-full kernel function for
k\geq 2
. We also examine the number of sign changes of
a(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n+1)
, where
n+1
runs over the sum of
m
squares. Here
m=2,4,6,8,10,12
.

In particular, we prove

Theorem 1
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}
and let
q\geq 100
be any integer. Then for any
\epsilon>0
and
q\ll x^{\frac{1}{j+1}-\epsilon}
, where
j\geq 2
is a fixed integer, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}\leq x+1\\
a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}\equiv 1~(\mathrm{mod}~q)\\
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2})=O\left(\frac{x^{1+\epsilon-\frac{1}{j+1}}q^{1+\epsilon}}{\phi(q)}\right).

Theorem 2
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}
and let
q\geq 100
be any integer. Then for any
\epsilon>0
and
q\ll x^{\frac{2}{j+1}-\epsilon}
, where
j\geq 2
is a fixed integer and for
m\in\{4,6,8,10,12\}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}\leq x+1\\
a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}\equiv 1~(\mathrm{mod}~q)\\
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2})=O\left(\frac{x^{\frac{m}{2}+\epsilon-\frac{2}{j+3}}q^{\frac{j+1}{j+3}+\epsilon}}{\phi(q)}\right).

Theorem 3
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}
and let
q\geq 100
be any integer. Then for any
\epsilon>0
and
q\ll x^{\frac{1}{(j+1)^{2}}-\epsilon}
, where
j\geq 2
is a fixed integer, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}\leq x+1\\
a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}\equiv 1~(\mathrm{mod}~q)\\
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2})=\frac{c_{j,f}}{q}x+O\left(\frac{x^{1+\epsilon-\frac{1}{(j+1)^{2}}}q^{1+\epsilon}}{\phi(q)}\right),

where
c_{j,f}
is a constant that depends on
j
and
f
.

Theorem 4
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}
and let
q\geq 100
be any integer. Then for any
\epsilon>0
and
q\ll x^{\frac{2}{(j+1)^{2}}-\epsilon}
, where
j\geq 2
is a fixed integer and for
m\in\{4,6,8,10,12\}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}\leq x\\
a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}\equiv 1~(\mathrm{mod}~q)\\
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2})=\frac{c_{m,j,f}}{q}x^{\frac{m}{2}}+O\left(\frac{x^{\frac{m}{2}+\epsilon-\frac{2}{(j+1)^{2}}}q^{1+\epsilon}}{\phi(q)}\right),

where
c_{m,j,f}
is a constant that depends on
j
,
f
and
m
.

Theorem 5
.

For any integer
k\geq 2
, let
a(n)
be a
k
-full kernel function and
f\in S_{\kappa}
. Let
j\geq 2
be an integer. Then for any
\epsilon>0
and
m\in\{4,6,8,10,12\}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x\\
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
n=a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}-1\end{subarray}}a(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n+1)=O\left(x^{\frac{m}{2}-\frac{2k-2}{3k(j+1)}+\epsilon}\right),

and also

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x+1\\
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
n=a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}-1\end{subarray}}a(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n+1)=O\left(x^{1-\frac{k-1}{3k(j+1)}+\epsilon}\right).

Theorem 6
.

For any integer
k\geq 2
, let
a(n)
be a
k
-full kernel function and
f\in S_{\kappa}
. Let
j\geq 2
be an integer. Then for any
\epsilon>0
and
m\in\{4,6,8,10,12\}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x+1\\
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
n=a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}-1\end{subarray}}a^{2}(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(n+1)=D_{j,f,m}x^{\frac{m}{2}}+O\left(x^{\frac{m}{2}-\frac{2k-2}{3(j+1)^{2}k}+\epsilon}\right),

and also

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x+1\\
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
n=a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}-1\end{subarray}}a^{2}(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(n+1)=D_{j,f,2}x+O\left(x^{1-\frac{k-1}{3(j+1)^{2}k}+\epsilon}\right),

where
D_{j,f,m}
and
D_{j,f,2}
are constants that can be evaluated explicitly.

Theorem 7
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}
and
j\geq 2
be a fixed integer and
a(n)
be a
k
-full kernel function for any integer
k\geq 2
. Then, for sufficiently large
x
, the sequence

\{a(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n+1)~|~n=a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\}

has at least
x^{1-\delta_{j}}
sign changes between
x
and
2x
, for any
\delta_{j}
with
1-\frac{k-1}{3k(j+1)^{2}}<\delta_{j}<1
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}
and
j\geq 2
be a fixed integer,
m\in\{4,6,8,10,12\}
and
a(n)
be a
k
-full kernel function for any integer
k\geq 2
. Then for sufficiently large
x
, the sequence

\{a(n)\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n+1)~|~n=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}^{2}-1,a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\}

has at least
x^{1-\delta_{j}}
sign changes between
x
and
2x
, for any
\delta_{j}
with
1-\frac{2k-2}{3k(j+1)^{2}}<\delta_{j}<1-\frac{k-1}{3k(j+1)^{2}}
.

Organisation of the article

This article is organised as follows. In Section
2
, we discuss the preliminaries, important lemmas, and bounds necessary to prove our results. The proofs of our main results, related to the partial sums of the symmetric power
L
-function attached to Hecke eigenforms, are given in Sections
3
,
4
,
5
, and
6
. Sections
7
and
8
deal with results related to shifted convolution sums of the symmetric power
L
-function twisted with
k
-full kernel functions. Finally, Sections
9
and
10
contain the results related to the number of sign changes of
\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n)
twisted with a
k
-full kernel function over the sum of squares.

2.
Preliminaries and some important lemmas

Let

r_{k}(n):=\#\bigl\{(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}:n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+\cdots+n_{k}^{2}=n\bigr\},

where we count all ordered
k
-tuples of integers
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})
satisfying the equation,
including zeros and treating different signs and permutations as distinct.

We will define the functions
r_{m}(n)
, where
m=2,4,6,8,10,12
, which are defined as follows

Definition 9
.

[
4
, p. 121]

For any positive integer
n
, define

(8)

\displaystyle r_{2}(n)=4\sum_{d|n}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(d),

(9)

\displaystyle r_{4}(n)=8\sum_{d|n}d,

(10)

\displaystyle r_{6}(n)=16\sum_{d|n}d^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)-4\sum_{d|n}d^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(d),

(11)

\displaystyle r_{8}(n)=16\sum_{d|n}(-1)^{n+d}d^{3},

(12)

\displaystyle r_{10}(n)=\frac{64}{5}\biggl\{\sum_{d|n}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(d^{\prime})d^{4}+\frac{1}{16}\sum_{d|n}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(d)d^{4}\biggr\}+\frac{32}{5}a_{n},

(13)

\displaystyle r_{12}(n)=8\sum_{d|n}(-1)^{n+d+\frac{n}{d}-1}d^{5}+16b_{n},

where
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}
is the non-principal Dirichlet character modulo
4
, i.e.,

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(n)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{4},\\
-1&\text{if }n\equiv-1\pmod{4},\\
0&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{2},\end{cases}

a_{n}
is defined via the identity

\theta_{2}^{4}\,\theta_{3}^{2}\,\theta_{4}^{4}=16\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}q^{n}\qquad(q=e^{2\pi iz}),

where the classical theta functions are given by

\theta_{2}=2q^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{2m})(1+q^{2m})^{2},

\theta_{3}=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{2m})(1+q^{2m-1})^{2},\text{ and}

\theta_{4}=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{2m})(1-q^{2m-1})^{2}\quad(|q|<1).

and
b_{n}
is defined via the identity

\left(\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}{\pi}\right)^{4}=16\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_{n}q^{n}\qquad(q=e^{2\pi iz}),

where

\theta_{1}^{\prime}=2\pi q^{1/4}\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{2m})^{3}\quad(|q|<1).

Define the arithmetic functions

Definition 10
.

(14)

\displaystyle l_{1}(n)=\sum_{d|n}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(d),

(15)

\displaystyle l_{2}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d,

(16)

\displaystyle l_{3}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right),\quad v_{3}(n)\sum_{d|n}d^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(d),

(17)

\displaystyle l_{4}(n)=\sum_{d|n}(-1)^{n+d}d^{3}.

(18)

\displaystyle l_{5}(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\!\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)d^{\,4},\qquad v_{5}(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(d)\,d^{\,4},

(19)

\displaystyle l_{6}(n)=\sum_{d|n}(-1)^{n+d+\frac{n}{d}-1}d^{5}.

Observing the definitions of the arithmetic functions, we have

(20)

\displaystyle r_{2}(n)=4l_{1}(n)\ll n^{\epsilon},

(21)

\displaystyle r_{4}(n)=8l_{2}(n)\ll n^{1+\epsilon},

(22)

\displaystyle r_{6}(n)=16l_{3}(n)-4v_{3}(n)\ll n^{2+\epsilon},

(23)

\displaystyle r_{8}(n)=16l_{4}(n)\ll n^{3+\epsilon},

(24)

\displaystyle r_{10}(n)=\frac{64}{5}\,l_{5}(n)+\frac{4}{5}\,v_{5}(n)+\frac{32}{5}\,a_{n},

(25)

\displaystyle r_{12}(n)=8l_{6}(n)+16b_{n}.

where
\epsilon>0
and we note that in general,
r_{m}(n)\ll n^{\frac{m}{2}-1}+\epsilon
for
m=2,4,6,8,10,12
.

We have

(26)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}\leq x\\
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}^{2}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2}\\
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{10}\end{subarray}}1

(27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{m}(n)

Now using (
20
), (
21
), (
22
), (
23
), (
24
), (
25
) and above equation, we have

(28)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{2}(n)=4\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)l_{1}(n),

(29)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{4}(n)=8\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)l_{2}(n),

(30)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{6}(n)=16\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)l_{3}(n)-4\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)v_{3}(n),

(31)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{8}(n)=16\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)l_{4}(n),

(32)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{10}(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{64}{5}\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)l_{5}(n)+\frac{4}{5}\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)v(n)

(33)

\displaystyle+\frac{32}{5}\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)a_{n},

(34)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)r_{12}(n)

\displaystyle=8\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)l_{6}(n)+16\sum_{n\leq x}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)b_{n}.

2.1.
Bounds for
\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n)

Note that (
2
) yields
\left|1-\frac{\alpha^{j-1}(p)\beta^{i}(p)}{p^{s}}\right|\geq 1-\frac{1}{p^{\sigma}}>0
for
\Re(s)=\sigma>1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{j}f)|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{p}\prod_{i=0}^{j}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{\sigma}}\right)^{-1}=\prod_{i=0}^{j}\zeta(\sigma)=\zeta(\sigma)^{j+1}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{j+1}(n)}{n^{\sigma}},

where
d_{j+1}(n)
is the number of ways of expressing
n
as a product of
j+1
factors.
Since
d_{k}(n)\leq d(n)^{k-1}
for positive integers
k
and
n
, and since
d(n)\ll_{\epsilon}n^{\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
, we obtain

d_{k}(n)\ll_{k,\epsilon}n^{\epsilon}\quad\text{for any }\epsilon>0.

Therefore, the Dirichlet series for
L(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f)
is absolutely convergent for
\Re(s)>1
.

Note that (
1
) and (
2
) imply

|\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)|\leq d_{j+1}(n).

Consequently, for any
\epsilon>0
,

(35)

|\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)|\ll_{j,\epsilon}n^{\epsilon}.

Since
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
is multiplicative,
L(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f)
admits an Euler product

(36)

L(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f)=\prod_{p}\Bigl(1+\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p)}{p^{s}}+\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p^{2})}{p^{2s}}+\dots\Bigr),

absolutely convergent for
\Re(s)>1
.

Observe that

(37)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(p)=\sum_{m=0}^{j}\alpha^{j-m}(p)\beta^{m}(p).

Moreover, Hecke theory gives the relation

(38)

\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p)=\lambda_{f}(p^{j})\qquad(\text{$p$ prime}),

and we also know that

(39)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(p)\;=\;1\;+\;\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{2\ell}f}(p).

2.2.
Important lemmas

Lemma 11
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
and let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,l_{1}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>1,

where
l_{1}(n)
is given by (
14
). Then
F_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr),

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}
is the unique non-principal Dirichlet character modulo
4
,
and
H_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

We know that,

\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,l_{1}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)\ll n^{\epsilon}\qquad(\epsilon>0),

which implies that the Dirichlet series
F_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
converges absolutely for
\Re(s)>1
. Since
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
is multiplicative,
F_{j}^{1}(s)
therefore admits an Euler product in this half-plane in
\Re(s)>1
:

(40)

\displaystyle F_{j}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\prod_{p}\Bigl(1+\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p)\,l_{1}(p){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)}{p^{s}}+\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p^{2})\,l_{1}(p^{2}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{2})}{p^{2s}}+\cdots+\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p^{m})\,l_{(}p^{m}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}+\cdots\Bigr).

Now define the multiplicative function
b_{1}(n)
via its Euler product

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}(n)}{n^{s}}:=L(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\;L(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})(=:G_{j}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})),

so that in particular for primes
p
,

b_{1}(p)=\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p)\,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)+\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p)\,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p).

Since
l(p)=1+{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(p)
, we obtain that
b_{1}(p)=\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(p)\,l_{1}(p){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(}p)
, establishing the desired equality at each prime.

But note that
b_{1}(p^{k})\neq\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(p^{k})l(p^{k}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{k})\text{ for }k>1
and again

\displaystyle|b_{1}(n)|=|(\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}*\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{d|n}|\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(d){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(d)||\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{d|n}d^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)^{\epsilon}\leq n^{\epsilon}d(n)\ll_{\epsilon}n^{\epsilon}\text{ for any }\epsilon>0.

So
\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}(n)}{n^{s}}
is absolutely convergent by
\Re(s)>1
and the Euler product ensures that

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}(n)}{n^{s}}=\prod_{p}\left(1+\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{b_{1}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}\right)\quad(\Re(s)>1).

Now,

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{p^{\epsilon m}}{p^{m\sigma}}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{p^{\epsilon m}}{p^{(1+2\epsilon)m}}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{p^{m(1+\epsilon)}}=\frac{1}{p^{1+\epsilon}-1}<1

for
\Re(s)>1+2\epsilon
.

Let

A=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(p^{m})l_{1}(p^{m}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}},\quad\text{and}

B=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}\quad(|B|<1).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1+A}{1+B}

\displaystyle=(1+A)(1-B+B^{2}-\cdots)

\displaystyle=1+A-B-AB+\cdots

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(p^{2})l_{1}(p^{2}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{2})-b_{1}(p^{2})}{p^{2s}}+\cdots+\frac{c(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}+\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{c_{p}(n)}{n^{s}}\quad\text{(say)},

where

c_{p}(n)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }n=1,\\
c(n)&\text{if }n=p^{m}\,(m\geq 2),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that the above equality holds for
\Re(s)>1+2\epsilon
for all
\epsilon>0
, and that the series is absolutely convergent in this region.
Also note that
c_{p}(n)\ll n^{\epsilon}
for all
\epsilon>0
.
We define
c(n)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
by

\prod_{p}\frac{1+A}{1+B}=\prod_{p}\left(1+\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{c(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c(n)}{n^{s}}.

By construction,
c(n)
is multiplicative.

Now Define

\displaystyle H_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=\frac{F_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})}{G_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{p}\frac{1+\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(p^{m})l_{1}(p^{m}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}}{1+\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{b(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{p}\frac{1+A}{1+B}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c(n)}{n^{s}}.

We now show the region of convergence of
H_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
:

Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{m\geq 3}\bigg|\frac{c(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\geq 3}\frac{p^{m\epsilon}}{p^{m\sigma}}=\sum_{m\geq 3}\frac{1}{p^{m(\sigma-\epsilon)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2(\sigma-\epsilon)}(p^{\sigma-\epsilon}-1)}<\frac{1}{p^{2(\sigma-\epsilon)}}\text{ for }\epsilon>0,\text{ as small as possible}.

The above inequality of the series is true for
\Re(s)>1+\epsilon
.
Again,

\displaystyle\frac{c(p^{2})}{p^{2\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(p^{2})l_{1}(p^{2}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p^{2})-b(p^{2})}{p^{2\sigma}}

\displaystyle=O(\frac{p^{2\epsilon}}{p^{2\sigma}})=O(\frac{1}{p^{2\sigma-2\epsilon}}).

Now
\prod_{p}\left(1+\bigg|\frac{c(p^{2})}{p^{2s}}\bigg|+\sum_{m\geq 3}\bigg|\frac{c(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}\bigg|\right)=\prod_{p}\left(1+u_{p}\right)
is convergent if and only if
\sum_{p}u_{p}
is convergent, where
u_{p}=|\frac{c(p^{2})}{p^{2s}}|+\sum_{m\geq 3}|\frac{c(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}|
. Note that

\sum_{p}u_{p}\ll\sum_{p}\frac{1}{p^{2\sigma-2\epsilon}}

is absolutely convergent for
2\sigma-2\epsilon>1
i.e.,
\sigma>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
for
\epsilon>0
as small as possible.

So in this region
H_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\ll_{\epsilon}1
and
H_{j}^{(1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is absolutely convergent in
\Re(s)>\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

Proof of the following lemmas follows the same process as above. So we don’t provide the details of the proofs.

Lemma 12
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
and let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,l_{2}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>2,

where
l_{2}(n)
is given by (
15
). Then
F_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s-1,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

and
H_{j}^{(2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{3}{2}
.

Lemma 13
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
and let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,l_{3}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>3,

where
l_{3}(n)
is given by (
16
). Then
F_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s-2,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

and
H_{j_{1}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{5}{2}
.

Lemma 14
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
and let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,v_{3}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>3,

where
v_{3}(n)
is given by (
16
). Then
F_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L\!\bigl(s-2,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

and
H_{j_{2}}^{(3)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series converging absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{5}{2}
.

Lemma 15
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
and let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j}^{(4)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,l_{4}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>4,

is given by (
17
). Then

G_{j}^{(4)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s-3,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

is Dirichlet a series converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{7}{2}
.

.

f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,

n

of the

-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

). Then

and

is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{9}{2}
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
and let

be the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient of the
j^{th}
symmetric power
L
-function associated to
f
. Define

F_{j_{2}}^{(5)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}(n)v_{5}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\quad\Re(s)>5,

where
v_{5}(n)
is given by (
18
). Then
F_{j_{2}}^{(5)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j_{2}}^{(5)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j_{2}}^{(5)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})H_{j_{2}}^{(5)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

and
H_{j_{2}}^{(5)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges uniformly and absolutely in the half plane
\Re(s)>\frac{9}{2}
.

Lemma 18
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
and let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
.

Define

F_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)\,l_{6}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>6,

where
l_{6}(n)
is a given by (
19
). Then
F_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L\!\bigl(s-5,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{j}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

and
H_{j}^{(6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{11}{2}
.

Lemma 19
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(n)\,l_{1}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>1,

where
l_{1}(n)
is given by (
14
). Then
F_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\prod_{n=1}^{j}L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

and
H_{j}^{(*1)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

We observe that
\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(n)l_{1}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)
is multiplicative and hence

(41)

\displaystyle F_{j}^{(*1)}(s)=\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(p)l_{1}(p){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)}{p^{s}}+\cdots+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(p^{m})l_{1}(p^{m}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(}p^{m})}{p^{ms}}+\cdots\right).

Using (
38
) and (
39
), we note that,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(p)l_{1}(p){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{f}^{2}(p^{j})\left(1+{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(p)\right){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\sum_{l=1}^{j}\lambda_{f}(p^{2l})\right)\left(1+{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(p)\right){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\sum_{l=1}^{j}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{2l}f}(p)\right)\left(1+{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}(p)\right){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)

\displaystyle={\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)+{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)+\sum_{l=1}^{j}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{2l}f}(p){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)+\sum_{l=1}^{j}\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{2l}f}(p){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(p)

\displaystyle=:b_{1}^{*}(p).\quad\text{(say)}

From the structure of
b_{1}^{*}(p)
, we define the coefficients
b_{1}^{*}(n)
as

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{*}(n)}{n^{s}}=L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\prod_{n=1}^{j}L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

which is absolutely convergent in
\Re(s)>1
. We also note that,

\displaystyle\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{b^{*}(p)}{p^{s}}+\cdots+\frac{b^{*}(p^{m})}{p^{ms}}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\prod_{n=1}^{j}L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{4}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})

\displaystyle=G_{j}^{*}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),\quad\text{(say)}

for
\Re(s)>1
. Observe that
b_{1}^{*}(n)\ll_{\epsilon}n^{\epsilon}
for any small positive constant
\epsilon
. The rest will be exactly as in Lemma
11
.
∎

Lemma 20
.

Let
f
be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight
k
for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
let
\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}(n)
denote the
n
-th normalised Fourier coefficient
of the
j
-th symmetric power
L
-function attached to
f
and let
\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
q
.

Define

F_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\operatorname{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(n)\,l_{2}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad\Re(s)>2,

where
l_{2}(n)
is given by (
15
). Then
F_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
admits a factorisation

F_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\,H_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s-1,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\prod_{n=1}^{j}L\!\bigl(s,\operatorname{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)\;L\!\bigl(s-1,\operatorname{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}\bigr)

and
H_{j}^{(*2)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
is a Dirichlet series that converges absolutely and uniformly
in the half-plane
\Re(s)>\frac{3}{2}
.

.

Lemma 26
.

be a normalised primitive holomorphic cusp form of weight

for

\textstyle\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo

and let

-th normalised Fourier coefficient of the

symmetric power
L
-function associated to

. Define

F_{j}^{(*6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sym}^{j}f}^{2}(n)l_{6}(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(n)}{n^{s}},\quad\Re(s)>6,

where
l_{6}(n)
is given by (
19
). Then

admits a factorisation

F_{j}^{(*6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})=G_{j}^{(*6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})H_{j}^{(*6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}),

in which

G_{j}^{(*6)}(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}):=L(s-5,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})\prod_{n=1}^{j}L(s-5,\mathrm{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})L(s,\mathrm{sym}^{2n}f\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}})
